\begin{document}
\thispagestyle{plain}
{\sffamily\small\bfseries\color{accent}URIEL RESEARCH\hfill 23 NOVEMBER 2026}\par\vspace{13pt}
{\Huge\bfseries\color{ink}Exceptional bands and positive prime mass\par}\vspace{7pt}
{\Large\color{mute}What survives a failed arithmetic certificate, and what equal windows contain on the real character modulo four\par}\vspace{14pt}
\textbf{Abstract.} The pinned arithmetic argument removes finitely many integer obstructions by choosing a clean interval. We ask how much of that step is needed to bound the positive mass of the same prime sum. Two features survive the failure of the published weight cutoff. An exceptional integer at the upper endpoint of the finite sum is inactive. For active weights, unused exponent margin in earlier estimates recovers a strictly larger range, with different allowances for moving and fixed fields. Finite cardinality on its own gives no small-mass guarantee on a prescribed interval. We derive the sharp search range for a movable clean window in the cardinality-only model and a separate interpolation inequality when the scalar comparison is known at both ends of holes.

For the actual smoothed real character sum modulo four on \([256,4096]\), every factor-two band occupies a quarter of the logarithmic window. At square-root normalization their shares of positive mass range from \(4.77256460\%\) to \(47.48374447\%\). Both extrema concern continuous band placements. The four adjacent bands change ranking under a change of normalization, even though their arithmetic data are identical. These experiments price a loss of information at a specific interface. The paper distinguishes a formal scalar replay, a written source-derived extension, and a finite certificate for the actual prime sum.

\medskip\textbf{Reading the results.} Sections on the arithmetic comparison identify exactly where the missing estimate is used. The clean-window calculation quantifies the freedom to move. The prime experiment measures the cost at a fixed scale. The endpoint interpolation argument uses the actual shape of the smoothed sum to connect the two. The accompanying archive contains the protocols, programs, precise enclosures, source hashes and verification receipts.
\clearpage\tableofcontents
\bigskip
\subsection*{An example to carry through the paper}
Take a single exceptional integer, \(64\). A test of the form \(e\le x^2\Rightarrow e\le x\) fails for every \(8\le x<64\). The test therefore fails on all of \([8,8^{3/2}]\), although its input contains only one exceptional integer. Yet the definition of the test says nothing about the value of a prime sum at \(8\), \(9\), or any other point of that interval. We must find a mathematical link between these two kinds of information. Different parts of the paper establish different links, with their assumptions stated at each use.
\clearpage
\section{The question left by the positive-mass experiment}
The preceding experiment followed a hypothetical zero to a demand on the smoothed prime sum. Suppose a member of a fixed, positively weighted character family \(F\) has a zero in the critical strip with real part \(\beta>1/2\). For \(1/2<\theta<\beta\), \(\lambda>1\), write
\begin{equation}
 W_\theta(Y)=\int_Y^{Y^\lambda}[G_F(y)]_+y^{-1-\theta}\,dy,\qquad
 H_\theta(y)=G_F(y)/y^\theta,\qquad [z]_+=\max(z,0).
 \label{eq:baseline}
\end{equation}
The previously derived consequence of the localized Landau argument says that for any fixed \(0<b<\lambda(\beta-\theta)\), eventually \(W_\theta(Y)>Y^b\) for \emph{every} start \(Y\). Once \(F,\theta,\lambda,b\) have been fixed, the threshold can be placed before the choice of the next window. The arithmetic proof needs only arbitrarily late starts at which the mass has subpower growth. The original construction achieves this by proving \(H_\theta\le1\) point by point on selected intervals, with \(\lambda=3/2\) at the final comparison. We use the previous analytic consequence as an input to interpretation. Its Lean module and reproduction instructions are in the previous experiment. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/b7b5bc6e5e6814f654753fcbd5f45fecf370344c/research/uriel/experiments/zero-and-arithmetic-windows/evidence/analytic/WindowMass.lean}{[Positive-mass implication]}

This leaves a precise asymmetry. A bound at every point is a particularly effective way to bound an integral. Failure to prove the pointwise premise at some points does not settle what happens to the integral. The prime sum there could be small for another reason, the omitted points could carry little logarithmic length, or the construction could choose another window. Each possibility requires different evidence. If the arithmetic supplies only a finite set of exceptional \emph{integers}, it has established none of the three by that statement alone.

\subsection{Which hypothesis is being removed?}
Fix an outer scale, exceptional data, and the arithmetic parameters needed at the step under examination. Remove a single premise, the implication that every exceptional integer below the large cutoff lies below a smaller power of the ordinate. Leave the other named hypotheses in place whenever a theorem needs them. In particular the number field, local prime, degree, ranges of weights and choice of local host are not free parameters to replace after observing a bad point. This convention makes an implication falsifiable. If we removed every arithmetic assumption at once, a constructed counterexample would tell us little about the role of this test.

The variable \(x\) in this cutoff belongs to an arithmetic construction. The variable \(y\) in \eqref{eq:baseline} is an ordinate of a prime sum. In the purely scalar comparisons below the two can denote the same positive real. The full source has scale selection and a rescaling between some of its interfaces. No numeric interval in the prime experiment is asserted to be the image of an actual exceptional weight of a chosen number field. The question is first what those interfaces imply, then how the quantity they are meant to bound behaves in an instance where it can be computed. In particular, factor two in the finite test means \([s,2s]\). The clean arithmetic interval \([Y,Y^2]\) has an entirely different meaning. On the log axis the former has fixed width \(\log 2\); the latter has width \(\log Y\).

\subsection{Four contrasts that make the experiment decidable}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Inactive versus costed.} Track the first use of the exceptional-integer hypothesis. Determine whether the summation requests an endpoint and whether the estimate it uses for an active term has unused exponent margin. Checking the cutoff without following its consumer misses both questions.
\item \textbf{Prescribed versus selectable.} Hold the cardinality cap fixed, allow adversarial integer locations, and compare a prescribed interval with one chosen from an outer block. Determine the minimum range that guarantees a clean power window, including endpoint equality. Then allow partial overlap and derive the unavoidable contaminated fraction for one band.
\item \textbf{Width versus area.} Hold the character, normalization and observable fixed, give every band the same logarithmic width, and search continuously over its placement. Vary \(\theta\) in a separate sensitivity check. Positivity is imposed before integrating, exactly as in \eqref{eq:baseline}.
\item \textbf{Uncertified interior versus certified endpoints.} Keep both endpoint estimates for the genuine prime sum while allowing the interior comparison to fail. Use the ramp definition to bound the extra area. Test the sign and the sharp single-jump constant against actual prime powers and against a separate principal-term control.
\end{enumerate}
The first two experiments identify conditional arithmetic statements. The third certifies finite values of a real Dirichlet-character sum. The fourth gives a theorem about the ramp itself under an additional endpoint hypothesis. Their evidence has different scopes. In particular, an adverse integer arrangement that rules out a generic implication need not be realized by a cohomological field.

\subsection{What counts as an answer}
A positive result here is a bound stated with the information it needs. A negative result is an example satisfying the premises of the proposed shortcut while violating its conclusion. A failure of \texttt{Good} alone is a negative answer to neither of the interesting questions: it neither makes a cohomology group large nor forces the sum over primes upward. If the upstream estimate really loses quantitative information, we should be able to say what remains finite, which exponent is still available, and which additional ingredient would recover a mass bound. If all we can exhibit is a model family of arbitrary functions, the conclusion applies to that model class only.

This study also distinguishes two historical comparisons. Earlier experiments replayed the selector at synthetic centers and tested the common-host requirement. The present question concerns the cost of using a window that encounters a center. Separately, the preceding paper measured the full positive mass and the unique peak for the modulo-four character. Those results allow a consistency check on today's full integral. The band-by-band ranking and the continuously optimizing starts require more information than the earlier peak and total supplied. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/bafda6a5728eb08b601943f0c9cb1d38d27c03a9/research/uriel/experiments/joint-witness/COMPUTATION.md}{[Earlier selector experiment]} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/b7b5bc6e5e6814f654753fcbd5f45fecf370344c/research/uriel/experiments/zero-and-arithmetic-windows/README.md}{[Earlier prime-window experiment]}


\section{Which arithmetic estimate first needs a clean band?}
\label{sec:band-upstream}
Fix the target character, the two auxiliary primes, the outer scale and the data at one inner ordinate. A finite set of positive integers enters as an accounting device for possible large cohomology groups. Its members index twists or weights. The real variable of the smoothed prime sum indexes scale. There is no identity between these two variables. An integer changes what we can certify about an ordinate through an inequality connecting the integer to a power of the ordinate. Keeping these roles visible makes it possible to remove a hypothesis without silently removing every other estimate in the proof.

Write \(C_t>0\) for the cutoff exponent, \(\epsilon>0\) for the small loss, and \(C_K>0\) for the constant in the cohomology estimate. The proofs below the final assembly use \(C_K\ge1\). We use \(k\) for an exceptional integer. The project's files also use \(e\) for a page index depending on \(x\). A single number field or layer can be fixed while the sum over \(k\) varies. Confusing the page with its summation weight would assign the exception to a different mathematical object. At ordinate \(x\ge1\), the source predicate is
\[
     k\in\mathcal E,\quad k\le x^{C_t}
          \quad\Longrightarrow\quad k\le x^{\epsilon/(2C_K)}
          \qquad\text{for every }k .
\]
Our conditioning keeps the remaining range, field, degree, threshold, admissibility and compatibility data wherever we use them. This is a test of one particular implication. It does not assign numerical values to the selected fields or their exceptional centers.

\providecommand{\bandorig}[3]{\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/#1.lean\#L#2}{#3}}
\providecommand{\bandly}[3]{\bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/#1}{#2}{#3}}
\subsection{Finiteness, numerical size, and the cost of one exceptional weight}
The first arithmetic input supplies at most \(R'\le R\) centers \(s_i\) in a non-Archimedean normed field. For each cutoff \(Z\) the source collects integers \(1\le k\le Z\) that are strictly close to any center at the specified \(\ell\)-adic resolution. At most one integer per center qualifies. Taking their union bounds its cardinality by \(R\), allowing overlaps and empty sets. For integers outside the union, distance from each center gives the first bound on the length \(\mathrm{shaInv}\). This first bound has the form \(R(\max(m,j_0)+c+\lfloor\log_\ell Z\rfloor)\), where \(m\) is the layer index. The field hypotheses produce this useful uniform estimate on all the regular weights even at a real ordinate for which the global predicate fails. \bandly{SecondBound}{19-L30}{Definitions of the first bound and its input.} \bandly{Exc}{14-L42}{Construction of the exceptional integers and cardinality estimate.}

For an exceptional weight the proof takes a different route. It compares the cohomology length in the varying field with a length in a reference layer of fixed level \(j_0\), up to the additive allowance \(R(m-j_0)_+\). The reference field has degree \(n\), absolute discriminant \(D\), and the required roots of unity. An independent input named \texttt{LemmaK} controls the cohomology of this reference field. Suppressing the common field, primality and integer conditions in the display, the usable estimate is
\[
 \mathrm{shaInv}(k)\;\le\; R(m-j_0)_+
       +2\exp\!\left((2kn)^{C_K}\log(2D)\right).
 \tag{A}\label{eq:band-raw}
\]
The finiteness conclusion survives together with (A). In the source it follows by lifting the finite reference length out of the extended natural numbers and applying the second bound. Both operations are meaningful without a small upper bound on \(k\). It is the next comparison, which replaces the right side of (A) by an expression in \(x\) uniform over the relevant weights, that loses its published exponent. This identifies a quantitative obstruction to that particular comparison, even when all of the objects under discussion are finite. \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/GeneratorCountTuples/LemmaK}{9-L17}{Definition of the exponential upper bound.} \bandly{Klen}{23-L33}{Reference-layer finiteness and factor two.} \bandly{ImportsAlt}{20-L49}{Case split, comparison and use of the small weight.}

The location of this substitution matters. The same source lines produce the nonexceptional case from the first bound alone. They never need proximity information at a different weight. In the exceptional case the exact residual inequality still depends on \(k,n,D\). If those quantities were independently available and sufficiently small on a given range, one could use them directly in (A). The logical failure of the uniform predicate says neither that the cohomology length is large nor that the right side of (A) is attained. A counterexample that replaced the actual length by infinity would violate retained hypotheses.

There is also an exact endpoint improvement, independent of asymptotics. Write \(t(x)\) for the integer cutoff used in the sum. The source consumer requires a bound for \(1\le k<t(x)\). Thus any exceptional member equal to \(t(x)\), or greater, can be ignored for this call even if it falls below \(x^{C_t}\). For the concrete cutoff \(t=\lfloor x^{C_t}\rfloor\), the integer \(t\) is the only ambient endpoint of that kind for \(x^{C_t}\ge1\). As a control take \(x=256,\ C_t=2,\ \eta=1\), and \(\mathcal E=\{65536\}\). The source predicate fails. All 65,535 active weights, from 1 through 65,535, pass the weight check vacuously. The experiment enumerated them. For a scalable version, take integral \(x=n>1\), \(C_t=2\), \(\eta=1\), and \(\mathcal E=\{n^2\}\). Each sum again excludes its unique troublesome weight. This example instantiates the cutoff logic only. \bandly{Vvat}{7-L20}{Finite sums over the half-open integer interval.} \bandly{PartA}{68-L76}{The pointwise estimate actually queried for each summand.}

\subsection{A larger recoverable range of exceptional weights}
We can quantify the surviving information more precisely than a yes-or-no test. Let \(a\ge0\) denote a proposed dimensionless exceptional-weight exponent, and let \(r\ge0\) denote the exponent available for the logarithm of the discriminant. Assume that at each ordinate to be certified, all exceptional weights that occur in the sum obey \(k\le x^{a\epsilon/C_K}\). Suppose further that \(n\le n_0\) and \(\log(2D)\le d_0 x^{r\epsilon}\), with \(n_0,d_0\) fixed across the ranges in question. Constants in the argument may depend on the fixed field data, \(\epsilon\), and the chosen strict exponent margin. The elementary exponent calculation gives
\[
 (2kn)^{C_K}\log(2D)
 \ \le\ (2n_0)^{C_K}d_0\,x^{(a+r)\epsilon}.
 \tag{B}\label{eq:band-prod}
\]
The published substitution uses \(a=1/2,\ r=1/4\), producing \(3\epsilon/4\). The final summation budget is \(\exp(x^{4\epsilon/5})\). Between these two exponents is a usable margin. Our five supplemental Lean declarations include the real-power multiplication rule in (B), with independent constants and exponents. \bandly{Kexp}{6-L38}{The original choice and the multiplication of powers.} \bandly{Final}{6-L19}{The published final allowance.}

Why does the smaller exponent suffice for the entire sum? Above the common range threshold the regular contribution per weight is at most \(C(1+\log x)\). The same expression absorbs the additive layer-index term in (A). There are at most \(x^{C_t}\) active weights. Enlarging fixed constants as necessary gives the pre-summation estimate
\[
    x^{C_t}\left[C(1+\log x)+2\exp(Kx^{(a+r)\epsilon})\right].
 \tag{C}\label{eq:band-collector}
\]
For \(x\) sufficiently large the logarithm of (C) is at most \(C_1\log x+C_2x^{(a+r)\epsilon}\). For example, \(1+\log x\le 2x\) for \(x\ge1\), and the exponential is at least 1 after taking nonnegative constants. The fixed coefficient multiplying the resulting bound can also be absorbed in a multiple of \(\log x\). When \(a+r<4/5\), both terms are eventually at most half of \(x^{4\epsilon/5}\), after choosing a common threshold. Exponentiating gives exactly the published target. Uniformity means the same threshold works wherever these hypotheses on \(k,n,D\) hold with the specified constants. The set of eligible ordinates can be disconnected. This proof compares every summand that is actually consumed; it does not require a measurable choice of a different cohomological witness at each real point. \bandly{Point2}{24-L49}{Conversion of the layer and cutoff terms to a logarithmic allowance.} \bandly{Final}{48-L68}{Original polynomial and exponential absorption.}

At the interface where only the displayed discriminant power \(x^{\epsilon/4}\) is known, any fixed \(a<11/20\) therefore works. We can take \(1/2<a<11/20\) and recover exceptional weights between the published cutoff and the larger power. On this range the original predicate may fail. The retained numerical estimates still imply the desired even-layer bound. This is a real weakening of the scalar sufficient condition. It does not assert that any particular center of the arithmetic construction produces a weight in that range. At \(a=11/20\) a coefficient \(K>1\) in (C) prevents a uniform absorption argument of this form. When \(K=1\) the external polynomial factor still matters. This counterexample to an endpoint implication compares allowed upper envelopes. The available upper bound can be wasteful on actual cohomology.

The difference between strict and non-strict exponents can be seen without an asymptotic numerical extrapolation. Use \(\log_2 x=12800,\epsilon=1/64\) as an exactly represented scale. For \(a=21/40,\ 11/20,\ 23/40\), take \(r=1/4\) and compare the leading logarithm \(Kx^{(a+r)\epsilon}\) with \(x^{4\epsilon/5}\). For \(K=2\) the three ratios are respectively \(1/16,\ 2,\ 64\). At the middle exponent the ratios for \(K=1/2,1,2\) equal those three coefficients exactly. An analogous test for a fixed discriminant uses \(a=31/40,\ 4/5,\ 33/40\) and gives the same ratios. These computations test the exponent and coefficient convention, rather than the size of the uncomputed field constants. On a single finite scale even a favorable strict exponent can lose to an enormous unknown coefficient. Existence of a sufficiently late threshold is all the argument supplies.

\subsection{Moving discriminants, fixed discriminants, and the actual prime pair}
A single exponent for all fields overlooks another distinction in the source. The even branch takes input fields varying with \(q,d\). It fixes an auxiliary layer level and obtains a degree cap independent of the selected pair. The discriminant estimate from \texttt{layerdisc} accepts \emph{any} real \(w\ge1\) controlling \(\log(qd)\) and gives \(\log(2D)\le c_0w\). The coarse pair input in \texttt{partA} is immediately evaluated at \(w=x^{\epsilon/4}\). If one works solely with that interface, the \(11/20\) threshold above is the relevant strict margin. Changing a parameter after the constants have been selected requires following the dependencies back to the specific pair. \bandly{Layerdisc}{8-L13}{Degree and discriminant estimates with a free auxiliary bound.} \bandly{PartA}{45-L59}{Its application to the varying even field.}

There is more information for a pair actually supplied at scale \(V=X^{1/J'}\). The inequalities \texttt{q\_up} and \texttt{d\_up} give
\[
        \log(qd)\le V^{\epsilon/5}\bigl(1+(\log V)^2\bigr).
\]
Throughout the relevant initial range \(1\le V\le x\). For every fixed \(\sigma>0\) this is eventually bounded by \(C_\sigma x^{(1/5+\sigma)\epsilon}\), by dominance of any positive power over a squared logarithm. The added 1 in \(w=\max(1,\log(qd))\) can be absorbed into the same coefficient. The constants are chosen for fixed \(\epsilon\) and \(\sigma\), before the particular late pair. The raw pair therefore permits \(a<3/5\) on this branch: choose \(0<\sigma<3/5-a\) and use (B). In the test ledger \(a=59/100\) and \(r=41/200\) sum to \(159/200<4/5\). This refinement emerged from inspecting where the coarse pair bound is rounded. It was analyzed after the primary exponent controls were specified. At \(a=3/5\) the extra logarithms at this interface leave no strict margin in this derivation. \bandorig{CharacterBoxes/ExistsPageN}{27-L35}{The two original upper bounds on the primes.} \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/CMFieldExponentialBounds/AdmissibleOfPageN}{60-L75}{Pair scale and ordering before rescaling.}

For each odd prime in the finite set under consideration the proof of \texttt{partB} fixes a reference field and a layer \(L_2\) before the outer scale \(X\). Its discriminant is a fixed positive integer. The published code chooses \(c_0=\log(2|\mathrm{disc}\,L_2|)\) and then weakens that constant by multiplying it by \(x^{\epsilon/4}\) to fit the common lemma. Keeping the constant gives \(r=0\). Thus \(a<4/5\) suffices for the odd layer bound assuming the other inputs. Constants may depend on the chosen odd prime and reference field. Taking a maximum over the fixed finite list permits a common threshold where required. A field isomorphism transfers the bound to the actual layer used in the sum; an arbitrary independent field for each ordinate would not give the same uniformity argument. \bandly{PartB}{59-L75}{The fixed discriminant before the outer-scale quantifier.} \bandly{PartB}{76-L95}{Isomorphism transfer and final sum.}

All three strict bounds describe the scope of a particular numerical envelope. Arithmetic may further reduce relevant discriminants or cohomology sizes; this study provides no lower bounds on those quantities. A mixture of even and odd exceptions must be checked in its own component. An integer occurring in both components inherits both requirements. Taking the larger odd threshold as a single global allowance would silently weaken the moving-field premise. The original proof safely forms the union of the finite sets, applies one stronger predicate to it, then restricts the predicate to each component. This construction explains why the component-specific information must be recovered before passing to the undifferentiated union. \bandly{ThmBabOf}{76-L103}{Union of the actual component sets, restriction, and separate calls.}

\section{From a local layer estimate to a bound on positive area}
\label{sec:band-consume}
The improved weight cutoff returns a cohomological estimate at a specified ordinate. To see how far this goes, follow the proof in the direction of the prime sum. The interface named \texttt{IsBWindow} bundles a range assertion together with two numerical controls, traditionally called the \(a\) and \(b\) bounds, at every \(x\in[Y,Y^2]\). The even branch supplies the first control and the odd branches supply the second. The formal theorem \texttt{jointWin\_of} invokes its first input to select a particular \(Y\), then invokes its other input on that same window. Reusing a locally favorable bound from another prime pair, level, or window would break this use of the theorem. The listed large-prime requirements on \(q,d\) are thresholds in the pair variable. They do not remove seven real ordinates from the window. \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/PrimeSetBoundDataOfPrimitiveChar}{40-L49}{Whole-window interface.} \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/PrimeSetBoundDataOfPrimitiveChar}{145-L156}{Joint selection and same-window specialization.}

The proof body reveals something its input signature hides. In the theorem deriving height and vanishing from its pointwise form, the proof takes the range conditions once and then calls the window assertion at precisely the ordinate being studied. Only the odd-layer component at that ordinate is passed into the pointwise height theorem. The latter still requires a large host, admissible cutoffs, a field of the indicated type, and all requisite layer bounds for the finite list of primes. Once these are supplied, the comparison at \(x\) never queries a neighboring scale. The subsequent field adapter uses the even bound and this height-and-vanishing bound at \(x\), along with a separate minus-part estimate and conductor controls. Thus the body admits a localized reading, conditional on these other inputs being held fixed and meeting their thresholds. A black-box application of the whole-window theorem would continue to require its whole-window premise. \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/HeightAndVanishing/ThmHV}{14-L38}{The actual query at one ordinate.} \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/HeightAndVanishing/LayerLe}{39-L44}{The pointwise hypothesis and range of hosts.} \bandorig{AutomorphicVarieties/CMFieldExponentialBounds/AdmissibleOfPageN}{90-L113}{Where the two local bounds meet.}

The same discipline applies one level closer to the actual prime sum. In the comparison theorem, a usable estimate for the character family requires both explicit-formula compatibility and a quantitative arithmetic core. The core and explicit estimate must refer to the same host, retained set, and ordinate. The threshold precedes all these local choices. Goodness of every exceptional weight at a point cannot by itself manufacture that host, show the period data are correct, or establish the chain bound. Conversely, if compatible inputs are independently available there, the scalar comparison is pointwise and can be specialized there. An interpolation argument using two good endpoints must identify all its endpoint inputs; the fact that an integer no longer violates the \texttt{Good} predicate at either endpoint provides just one piece of that evidence. \bandorig{PrimitiveCosets/CoreGm}{12-L19}{The two compatible inputs to the scalar estimate.} \bandorig{PrimitiveCosets/WindowInequalityOfArtinInputs}{11-L25}{Selecting the local host.} \bandorig{PrimitiveCosets/LevelGmLeOnFamilyScales}{13-L30}{Identification of its retained data.}

\subsection{What the definition of the prime sum gives without the host}
A different inequality survives everywhere simply because the measured function has a concrete definition. For a fixed finite family of admissible characters \(F\), the pinned source proves a quadratic growth bound for individual summands, from which the corresponding family estimate follows by the triangle inequality. A sharper elementary envelope for our purpose is
\[
                      |G_F(y)| \le C_F y\log(2y),\qquad y\ge1.
 \tag{D}\label{eq:band-elem}
\]
Here the coefficient can depend on all characters, family weights and linear correction terms. To verify the order, let \(N=\lfloor y\rfloor\). For \(1\le n\le N\) we have \(\lfloor N/n\rfloor\ge N/(2n)\). The identity \(\sum_{d\mid m}\Lambda(d)=\log m\) follows by expanding the prime factorization of \(m\). Sum it over \(m\le N\) and interchange finite sums. This gives
\[
      \sum_{n\le N}\frac{\Lambda(n)}{n}
       \le\frac{2}{N}\sum_{n\le N}\Lambda(n)\lfloor N/n\rfloor
       =\frac{2}{N}\log(N!)\le2\log N .
\]
The ramp coefficient is at most \(y/n\), the absolute character values are at most one, and \(\Lambda(1)=0\). The principal correction contributes at most \(y\log y+|1+\gamma|y\), while the nonprincipal correction is a fixed multiple of \(y\). These give (D) including \(1\le y<2\). The argument needs no arithmetic witness or zero-free conclusion. It discards the cancellations among signed coefficients which stronger estimates exploit. \bandorig{DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily}{10-L36}{Ramp, correction and family normalization.} \bandorig{DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/GchiGrowth}{9-L22}{Original elementary quadratic estimate.}

For \(0<\theta<1\) and an exceptional set \(E\subset[Y,Y^\lambda]\) of real ordinates, (D) bounds the positive mass on \(E\) by
\[
      C_F Y^{\lambda(1-\theta)}\log(2Y^\lambda)\,
                             \int_E\frac{dy}{y}.
\]
The same constant works on arbitrarily late intervals for a fixed \(F\). The estimate has a clear price: to deduce subpower mass from this expression using length alone, one sufficient condition is logarithmic length at most \(Y^{-\lambda(1-\theta)+o(1)}\). This is a sufficient uniform estimate, not a necessary condition for the actual prime function to have small mass. A lower local height, cancellation before the positive part is formed, or more specific shape information could make much longer exceptional sets affordable. Integer cardinality supplies no such estimate for a prescribed location. The example in the next paragraph shows why the failure persists even for a continuous function of nearly linear growth and a family of arbitrarily late test windows.

\subsection{One fixed countermodel for the reduced scalar assumptions}
Use the genuine ranges \(C_t=401\), \(C_K=1\), and \(\epsilon=1/64<1/52\) in the scalar predicate; they do not specify an actual arithmetic host. Set \(U_j=4^j\) for \(j\ge3\), \(Y_j=2^{U_j}\), and in each instance take the single exceptional weight \(k_j=Y_j\). For every \(x\in[Y_j,Y_j^{3/2}]\),
\[
                 x^{1/128}<k_j\le x^{401}.
\]
One exceptional member therefore makes every point fail the scalar predicate on each prescribed window, including both endpoints. A cardinality bound uniform in \(j\) holds. The exception itself varies, as finite exception sets in the construction may depend on the outer scale. An argument holding the same integer fixed while moving \(Y\) beyond all thresholds would describe a different problem.

Choose a continuous function \(p\) supported on \([1,3/2]\), zero at its endpoints, linear from 0 to 1 on \([1,5/4]\), identically 1 on \([5/4,11/8]\), and linear down to zero thereafter. Define a \emph{single} signal \(G(x)=x\,p(\log_2(x)/U_j)\) on each of these disjoint windows, and zero elsewhere for \(x\ge1\). The definitions agree at boundaries. Thus \(|G(x)|\le x\) for all \(x\), it is zero off the prescribed windows, and the same observable is used at every scale. At \(\theta=1/2\), the plateau gives
\[
    \int_{Y_j}^{Y_j^{3/2}}\frac{[G(x)]_+}{\sqrt{x}}\frac{dx}{x}
                \ \ge\ \tfrac18(\log Y_j)\,Y_j^{5/8}.
\]
The lower estimate follows directly from the plateau width in natural log coordinates and its minimum normalized height. The finite exact checks use four outer log-starts from 64 to 512. The algebra covers the full infinite sequence, independently of how many rows are computed. This model shows that cardinality, a specified window, a bound off the bad portion of that window, continuity, and a growth envelope even stronger than (D) can coexist with power-size mass on that portion. It preserves neither arithmetic coefficients nor the other arithmetic witnesses, and does not claim to. The original selector may choose different, entirely safe intervals; indeed the model has quiet gaps of its own. A countermodel to a scalar implication must not be promoted to a counterexample to a theorem with different objects and stronger premises.

\subsection{What this experiment checked}
The portable source audit checked 25 complete Lean files against their Git blob identifiers at the published pin and verified 45 exact line spans. The numerical controls use integers and rational exponents, including endpoint equality, an active-set enumeration, free-constant stress tests at the exponent boundary, and the four prescribed-window instances. Five new declarations in the standalone \texttt{BandBudget} module were elaborated and replayed by Lean 4.33.1 against the pinned Mathlib revision. They establish the scalar product estimate (B), eventual absorption of a logarithmic overhead and any strictly lower power, failure of uniform endpoint absorption for a coefficient greater than one, the finite-sum active-index implication, and the moving-field exponent calculation. Their axiom inventory contains propositional extensionality, classical choice and quotient soundness. The field-level reassembly with the widened weight caps, the fixed-discriminant improvement and the sharper pair-range estimate are written deductions from the traced source. No modified upstream arithmetic module or complete RH theorem was compiled for this ablation.

The resulting distinction matters for the measurements that follow. A region excluded by the original sufficient certificate might still satisfy a pointwise arithmetic estimate through the active-index filter or the recovered exponent margin. A region outside all those known routes still has the elementary global estimate. On a finite interval an exact computation can reveal much smaller positive mass again. These three conclusions answer different questions and can simultaneously hold on the same region. A proof that the last behavior persists along an unbounded supply would require additional arithmetic information about the ordinates or their heights.


\section{How much freedom does a clean window require?}

\subsection{Three intervals that enter different measurements}
Fix an outer scale \(X>1\), exponents \(0<\eta\le C_t\), and a finite set of positive integer labels. The source requires, at each acceptable real argument \(x\), that every label \(e\) at most \(x^{C_t}\) already be at most \(x^\eta\). For \(e>1\) this comparison fails exactly when
\[
                 e^{1/C_t}\le x<e^{1/\eta}.
\]
A label equal to zero or one has no effect for \(x>1\). These endpoints matter because the source conclusion holds at every point of a closed power window. The lower endpoint is forbidden and the upper endpoint is available. The positive-mass integral assigns either endpoint zero mass. A selector for pointwise validity must retain a distinction that an integrator can safely forget. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/SecondBound.lean#L46-L48}{Pinned definition of the good-scale predicate.}

Write \(U=\log X\), \(u=\log x\), \(a=(\log e)/C_t\), and \(B=C_t/\eta\). In this coordinate the forbidden band is \([a,Ba)\). A factor-of-two band in the original argument, such as \([256,512]\), has constant length \(\log 2\) in \(u\). An arithmetic label instead cuts out length \((B-1)a\), which grows with its position. Consequently measured windows of equal logarithmic width provide controlled placements for a height-versus-location test. They cannot all be the full forbidden bands of the same \((C_t,\eta)\) at distinct labels. Nor would this identification be needed to test how a specified part of the fixed prime signal contributes to its integral.

There is a second change of variable that is useful only for the selector. In \(\log u\), every nonempty arithmetic band has width \(\log B\). The proposed clean window \([Y,Y^\lambda]\), for fixed \(\lambda>1\), now has width \(\log\lambda\). Translating this second interval changes its location freely. But \(d\log u\) also discounts a unit of logarithmic prime-sum area by a factor \(1/u\). Thus an equal-width problem for the selector becomes a nonuniform-area problem for the analytic criterion. Below, the second logarithm accounts for the freedom to choose a window. Whenever a mass or contamination fraction is reported, its measure is \(du=dx/x\).

The actual arithmetic union of exceptions is constructed once the outer scale and prime pair have been fixed. Its component cardinalities are added to form a cap \(R\). Each candidate \(Y\) is tested against that same union. The following minimax question preserves this order. Nature picks at most \(R\) labels at a given \(X\), then the selector sees them and chooses \(Y\). An adversary that chooses a new label after seeing each candidate \(Y\) would describe a different theorem. The uniform result ranges over arbitrary sets under the cap; the particular arithmetic construction can produce a more restricted family. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/ThmBabOf.lean#L75-L98}{Construction, choice of window, and use of the same exception union.}

\subsection{An exact outer-scale budget}
Let \(J\ge\lambda\) denote the search budget and require \(X^{1/J}\le Y\) and \(Y^\lambda\le X\). A larger \(J\) allows searching further toward the left while keeping the outer scale fixed. Set \(v=\log Y\). Candidate starts are the closed interval \([U/J,U/\lambda]\), and at such a start the observable is measured on \([v,\lambda v]\). Hence a further interval appears naturally: the set of \emph{starts} made unusable by a particular label. For \(a>0\) and \(B>1\),
\[
 [v,\lambda v]\cap[a,Ba)\ne\varnothing
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a/\lambda\le v<Ba .
\]
For the forward implication any point of intersection crosses both necessary boundaries. For the converse choose the larger of \(a\) and \(v\). The strict right inequality ensures the proposed point lies inside the half-open band. The extra factor \(\lambda\) describes how much further back a bad band can spoil a window that begins before it. Treating the band and its forbidden starts as identical would misstate the required budget even for a single label.

For \(B>1\), the least uniform budget over arbitrary sets of at most \(R\) labels is
\begin{equation}
            J_* = \lambda(\lambda B)^R.
            \label{eq:geometry-clean}
\end{equation}
First consider sufficiency, including equality. In \(\log v\) the closed range of possible starts has length \(\log(J/\lambda)\), whereas one forbidden set of starts is half-open of length \(\log(\lambda B)\). For \(J>J_*\), subadditivity of length proves the claim immediately. At equality, if the full closed interval were covered by finitely many half-open intervals with precisely its total length, the interval covering the right endpoint would extend positively beyond it. The remaining total length inside would then be insufficient. Equivalently one can sort the starts and move an initially admissible candidate to the free right endpoint each time a band blocks it. At most \(R\) such moves exhaust the list. When \(R=0\), the sole budget needed merely fits one power window. When \(B=1\), every band is empty and the least budget is again \(\lambda\), including cases with positive \(R\).

A separate induction supplies the upper bound in Lean without invoking a measure. If there is a center in \((U/(\lambda B),U]\), remove it temporarily and search below \(U/(\lambda B)\) with one fewer label. The found interval lies strictly to the left of the removed center, hence it need never be checked against that label again. If this interval of center positions is empty, the last candidate \(v=U/\lambda\) works: any relevant center below it has its right edge at or before \(v\). The induction's initial value is \(v=U/\lambda\). Each removal multiplies the accessible lower start by a further \((\lambda B)^{-1}\). This proves the first half of the sharp result even for extraneous nonpositive centers in the abstract real-index problem.

At \(\lambda=2\), the pinned selector uses \(2^{R+2}B^R\). Its induction starts with a permitted lower start of \(U/4\) when no centers remain, although it actually chooses \(U/2\). Carrying that last factor through the recurrence gives \(2^{R+1}B^R\) as in \eqref{eq:geometry-clean}. No new arithmetic input enters the improvement. For fixed \(X\) this doubles the guaranteed lower bound on \(\log Y\). It changes none of the original source declarations, and downstream arguments may use their chosen \(J\) for other purposes. The pinned proof works with a sufficient budget and requires no optimality claim at this step. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/Window1.lean#L6-L40}{Original recursion.} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/AutomorphicVarieties/LayeredIsomorphisms/Window.lean#L7-L23}{Transfer to the real arithmetic scale.}

\subsection{Adversarial centers and actual integers}
For necessity suppose \(R\ge1\), \(B>1\), and \(\lambda\le J<J_*\). Normalize the least start \(U/J\) to one, so the greatest start is \(r=J/\lambda\). Write \(K=\lambda B\). Since \(r<K^R\), choose a small positive \(s<1\) for which \(((1-s)K)^R>r\). Let the forbidden-start intervals begin at the \(R\) points
\[
           (1-s)\bigl((1-s)K\bigr)^j,
               \qquad 0\le j<R,
\]
and extend from each start by the multiplicative factor \(K\). Multiplying each left endpoint by \(\lambda\) recovers its center. Consecutive intervals overlap in their interiors, the first starts before one, and the last ends beyond \(r\). Thus each allowed window has positive-length intersection with an exceptional band. Geometric spacing forces the selector to spend its freedom crossing all the obstructions. Stacking the centers nearly on top of one another would make the same cardinality cap much less costly.

Real centers in this argument can also give integer-labeled counterexamples at arbitrarily large outer scales. In the coordinate \(u=\log x\), multiply all centers and all admitted starts by a common factor \(D\). Replace each intended center \(Da_j\) by \(\log\lceil\exp(C_tDa_j)\rceil/C_t\). The displacement is at most \(\exp(-C_tDa_j)/C_t\), while each strict overlap and endpoint margin grows in proportion to \(D\). For sufficiently large \(D\) the open cover persists. The integer labels depend on the outer scale in this construction. This rounding proves sharpness for arbitrary integer labels allowed solely by the cardinality cap; it supplies no evidence that the pinned cohomological construction can realize those labels.

A small example permits a literal hand check. Take \(C_t=2\), \(\eta=1\), \(\lambda=2\), \(R=1\), \(X=2^{56}\), and the single integer label \(e=2^{30}\). With logarithms to base two the bad band on the integration axis is \([15,30)\). At budget \(J=7\) the candidate starts are \([8,28]\). This whole interval lies in the interior of \([7.5,30)\), the set of starts spoiled by the single center. At \(J=8\), the start \(7\) gives the entirely clean interval \([7,14]\). These equalities concern the transformed variables, so they require no table of primes. Two and three labels similarly give exact failures at \(J=31\) and \(127\) with thresholds \(32\) and \(128\); the receipt records the labels as powers of two to avoid printing enormous integers.

There is a useful discontinuity in the result. As \(B\) decreases to one from above, the worst-case budget in \eqref{eq:geometry-clean} approaches \(\lambda^{R+1}\). At \(B=1\) it falls to \(\lambda\). A band of arbitrarily small positive length can exclude a whole range of starts for windows that must avoid every point of it. Its contribution to an integral of bounded height, however, tends to zero with its length. This difference reflects the task posed to the selector and will reappear in the quantitative area calculation.

\section{What partial contamination costs}
\subsection{The sharp local price of a missing estimate}
For a fixed candidate window let \(L\) be its length in \(u=\log x\), and let \(m\) be the length of its intersection with the union of bands. Suppose usable information bounds the normalized real observable by \(C\) off the bands and by \(M\) on them, with \(0\le C\le M\). The best general upper bound on positive mass from those data is
\[
                     C(L-m)+Mm.
\]
It follows by splitting the integral over disjoint sets. A measurable signal taking respectively the two constant values attains equality; for finitely many clipped bands, continuous signals with short linear transition regions approach it. This statement concerns scalar envelopes. A particular prime signal may lie far below either ceiling or may be negative across a substantial part of the interval. The certified prime measurements address those features directly.

When \(M\) is unspecified, one interval of positive overlap permits arbitrarily large area in the envelope model. The same holds \emph{simultaneously} for all candidate windows in the strict chain cover just constructed. Use finitely many continuous nonnegative tent functions, each positive in the interior of one bad band and zero on its boundary and outside. Their sum has positive integral on each candidate window; its integral depends continuously on the start, as follows by evaluating an antiderivative at the two moving endpoints. On the compact candidate-start range its minimum is strictly positive. Multiplying all tents by the same constant now makes that minimum as large as desired while leaving all off-band comparisons intact. A separate signal for each possible window would establish a weaker quantifier order. A point-only intersection, such as \([1,2]\) meeting \([2,4)\) at 2, gives zero area and cannot support this construction.

The consequence is local to one outer search block. It applies equally at a sequence of increasing blocks chosen with disjoint interiors by setting new amplitudes on each. It is insufficient to conclude that every sufficiently late window of some genuine fixed arithmetic family has high mass, or even that all possible outer blocks fail. If the source always provides the full clean-window budget, one successful window in each sufficiently large block already meets the cofinal requirement. The counterexamples explain precisely why cardinality and off-band control alone cannot support an integral shortcut inside an arbitrarily prescribed smaller block.

\subsection{A sharp partial-cover formula for one label}
A height cap allows a more discriminating experiment. Normalize the least log start to one and the greatest to \(r=J/\lambda\ge1\). For one possible center \(a\), define
\[
 Q(a,v)=\frac{|[v,\lambda v]\cap[a,Ba)|}{(\lambda-1)v},
                    \qquad 1\le v\le r.
\]
Lengths here are ordinary lengths in the logarithmic integration variable, and the denominator normalizes for the longer windows available further right. The worst unavoidable contamination fraction is \(Q_* = \sup_{a>0}\inf_{1\le v\le r}Q(a,v)\). Nature moves one band; the selector then chooses the window with the smallest contaminated fraction. For \(B>1\) the exact value is
\begin{equation}
 Q_* = \frac{1}{\lambda-1}\max\!\left(0,\min\!\left\{
 \lambda-1,\frac{B\lambda-r}{B+r},
               \frac{\lambda(B-1)}{r+B-1}\right\}\right).
 \label{eq:geometry-fraction}
\end{equation}
At \(B=1\) the value is zero. This formula answers a fractional-area question. Minimizing absolute positive mass over moving windows introduces an additional factor of \(v\) and need not choose the same start.

To obtain \eqref{eq:geometry-fraction}, divide all endpoints by the candidate start \(v\), and set \(t=a/v\). In these units the window is \([1,\lambda]\) and the band is \([t,Bt)\). A positive amount \(k\) fits in their intersection exactly when all four distances from an upper endpoint to a lower endpoint are at least \(k\). Two of the distances are the respective widths. The other two account for which interval begins later and which ends first. They give
\[
 k\le\lambda-1,\qquad
       \max\!\left\{\frac{1+k}{B},\frac{k}{B-1}\right\}
                \le t\le\lambda-k .
\]
At fixed \(a\) the entire range of \(t\) has lower endpoint \(a/r\) and upper endpoint \(a\). Some center forces \(k\) at every start if and only if \(r\) times the displayed lower endpoint bound is at most \(\lambda-k\). Solving the resulting two linear inequalities and imposing the window-width restriction gives the three terms in the minimum. If they admit no positive \(k\), the fraction is zero. Otherwise \(a=\lambda-k\) at the maximal \(k\) is a possible maximizing center. The derivation also shows why only the extreme starts need checking for a fixed proposed fraction: the superlevel set in the variable \(a/v\) is a single interval. Positive mass for a general sign-changing prime function has no comparable superlevel property.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrr}
\toprule
Search budget \(J\) & 2 & 3 & 4 & 6 & 8 \\
\midrule
Worst unavoidable fraction, \(B=\lambda=2\)
    & 1 & \(5/7\) & \(1/2\) & \(1/5\) & 0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
At \(J=4\), for example, the normalized search allows starts from one to two. Placing the band at \([3/2,3)\) clips exactly half the window at each extreme start and at least half at every intermediate start. The original arithmetic does not require accepting such an interval. The example shows how much independent height control would be doing if the selector were restricted to that range. Even at \(J=6\) a fixed fraction can be forced, so an envelope growing as a positive power just on the bad part can still cost a positive power on the best available window.

The approach to the clean threshold is regular for this fractional question. With \(B=\lambda=2\), set \(r=4(1-\xi)\) for small positive \(\xi\). Then \(Q_*=4\xi/(6-4\xi)\). If the accessible budget approaches the clean threshold at a power rate in the outer scale, the optimized fraction decreases at that rate. Multiplication by the whole window length, of order \(\log X\) in this fixed-budget example, converts that decrease into a bound on the exceptional logarithmic length. An independent height bound can then be compared directly to this length. The calculation quantifies the cost of requiring a slightly higher lower endpoint for the search. The upstream cohomological input demonstrated here supplies a cardinality bound and clean selection, without such a separate height budget on every exceptional ordinate.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\bandfigure{.96\linewidth}{range}\caption{Worst unavoidable overlap with one exceptional band when the dilation and window exponent both equal two. The selector sees the label before choosing the window. The symbols mark exact rational values from the independent geometry calculation.}\end{figure}

\subsection{A second way to use partial coverage}
There is a related way to salvage a prescribed window that pays no price for the height of an excursion. Suppose one already knows that a set of missing comparisons occupies absolute logarithmic length \(m\) in \([U,\lambda U]\) and has at most \(k\) interval components. The remaining set consists of at most \(k+1\) gaps. On each of these the desired one-sided estimate holds for the same character family. A sufficiently long single gap is valuable because the analytic obstruction from a hypothetical zero applies on any fixed positive power-width window, including one extracted from within a larger arithmetic window.

One can give explicit constants. The total length of all the good gaps in the \(\log u\) coordinate is at least
\[
             \log\!\left(\frac{\lambda}{1+m/U}\right).
\]
Indeed the integral of \(1/u\) over the bad set of length \(m\) is largest when all of it is placed at the left edge of \([U,\lambda U]\). To verify this assertion, compare the removed part inside \([U,U+m]\) with the replacing part outside it. They have equal ordinary measure, and every integrand value on the former is at least every value on the latter. Subtract the resulting \(\log(1+m/U)\) from \(\log\lambda\). One good gap therefore has ratio of upper to lower endpoint at least
\[
                  \left(\frac{\lambda}{1+m/U}\right)^{1/(k+1)}
\]
in the \(u\) coordinate. Any strictly smaller ratio greater than one fits inside the gap even if both boundaries must be avoided. Returning to the original ordinate gives a closed power subwindow of this exponent. If \(k\) is uniformly bounded and the uncovered fraction \(m/((\lambda-1)U)\) stays uniformly below one, a fixed positive width persists on arbitrarily late windows. No bound on the prime sum inside the discarded components enters this particular conclusion.

These two requirements constrain different failures. A bounded number of bad components can still fill the entire prescribed window, as the single-label example shows. Small bad length alone can also leave no fixed-width clean subwindow. In a synthetic interval \([U,2U]\), split the length into \(n\) equal parts and place a short bad interval of length \(1/n^2\) at each interior division point. Take \(U=2^n\). Total bad length tends to zero, and the ratio of the endpoints of any clean gap is at most \(1+1/n\). An area argument with bounded height may still succeed there. This example concerns unrestricted exceptional intervals; it does not assign independent shrinking widths to the fixed-ratio arithmetic label bands. The follow-up enumeration checked the gap inequality on every one of the 1,024 ten-cell masks. Only the empty and full masks attained equality on that grid. At \(n=64\) the fragmented construction removed total logarithmic length \(63/4096\), yet the best clean gap had upper-to-lower endpoint ratio only \(65/64\) in the log coordinate. The preceding bounds distinguish two possible upstream discoveries. One might control the height-weighted mass on the exceptional set, or one might control its location and fragmentation enough to extract a quantitatively useful gap while permitting arbitrary heights inside it.

\subsection{Independent calculations and scope of the certificate}
The first finite test code directly checks whether \(\max(v,a)\) is a point of intersection and searches candidates at the leftmost allowed start, the rightmost start, and each free band endpoint. The second code translates bands to intervals of forbidden starts, sorts these intervals, and sweeps the candidate to the right until no interval covers it. The two exact-rational oracles agreed across 9,732 configurations. Of these, 6,960 lay at or above the predicted threshold and each admitted a clean window. Only 24 of the grid configurations blocked every choice. The low prevalence on this particular grid explains the value of constructing adversaries: geometrically spaced strict overlaps produced 36 additional below-threshold obstructions. Grid prevalence has no probabilistic interpretation for the arithmetic labels.

The independent check of the partial fraction uses the two endpoint windows. Their individual overlap fractions are piecewise affine in the center. Slicing the center axis at every coincidence of a geometric endpoint produces intervals on which both fractions are affine. The maximum of the smaller fraction can occur at an endpoint or where those affine functions cross. Exact evaluation agreed with \eqref{eq:geometry-fraction} in all 180 parameter triples; 94 had strictly positive unavoidable contamination. At 3,060 intermediate placements the maximizing center attained at least the predicted lower fraction. A separate endpoint control found a pointwise failure of zero measure and a wholly clean window starting precisely at the excluded right edge. An exact triangular-pulse control changed 40 units of positive area to zero upon sign reversal at the same locations and absolute heights. Together these controls isolate the roles of the selector, the envelope, and the positive-part statistic.

The supplemental file \texttt{WindowGeometry.lean} formalizes the hit criterion, the uniform sufficient budget, the four inequalities for a positive overlap length, obstruction by a geometric chain, and the complete feasibility equivalence for a positive candidate \(k\) in the one-band minimax problem. The latter equivalence gives precisely the three inequalities used to read off \eqref{eq:geometry-fraction}. All five declarations were elaborated with Lean 4.33.1 and independently kernel-replayed against the pinned Mathlib. The construction of sharp parameters, the integer rounding argument, the passage to a continuous high-area signal and the gap-extraction inequality are written mathematical deductions. The rational receipts supply independent finite checks at the indicated parameters. The global proof does not require any of the improvements, and sharpness in the arbitrary-label class imposes no equality condition on actual cohomological exceptional sets.


\section{What an equal length of genuine prime data can contain}
\label{sec:band-prime-design}

Suppose an arithmetic argument has located an interval on which it cannot certify its usual bound. It knows the interval's length on a logarithmic axis. Even if one also knows the exact height of the largest peak in the surrounding prime sum, how much of the positive integral might lie there? We study this question on one real, continuously specified prime sum, where the entire curve and the integral over every possible band admit numerical certificates. A band is an interval on which we deliberately inspect the signal. Its placement below does not assert the existence of an exceptional center for a number field. This distinction permits us to answer the measurement question without assigning invented arithmetic data to the proof.

Use the primitive real character modulo four, with values zero on even numbers, one on integers congruent to one modulo four, and minus one on integers congruent to three modulo four. Write $\Lambda$ for the von Mangoldt function and let $c_4=L'(1,\chi_4)/L(1,\chi_4)$. The real smoothed observable at $y\geq 1$ is
\[
 G(y)=c_4y+\sum_{n\leq y}\chi_4(n)\Lambda(n)(y/n-1).
\]
The sign of the correction is important. It cancels the contribution at the linear scale in the intended normalization. The inherited computation of $c_4$ uses the Euler transformation of the alternating Dirichlet beta series and its logarithmic derivative. It encloses the transformed tails and checks the resulting interval against the independent bounds obtained from ordinary alternating partial sums. Here we use 80 Euler-transform terms in the primary calculation and 55 in a numerical control. The primitive for the entire experiment remains the same real function. Changing a band, or even changing the power used to weight it, does not change its character, its coefficients or its normalization. The detailed derivation of the normalization and the earlier whole-window computation are available in the precursor paper. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/b7b5bc6e5e6814f654753fcbd5f45fecf370344c/research/uriel/experiments/zero-and-arithmetic-windows/A_Zero_and_the_Prime_Windows.pdf}{[A zero and the prime windows, Sections 9--10]}

The parent interval is $[256,4096]$. For $256\leq s\leq2048$ we measure
\[
 M_\theta(s)=\int_s^{2s}[G(y)]_+ y^{-1-\theta}\,dy,
 \qquad T_\theta=\int_{256}^{4096}[G(y)]_+y^{-1-\theta}\,dy.
\]
Each band has logarithmic length $\log 2$, precisely one quarter of the parent length $\log 16$. If positive mass were uniformly distributed in $\log y$, each band would contain a quarter of the total. This supplies a scale for interpreting the measured fractions. No model of randomness or uniform distribution enters the calculations. We set $\theta=1/2$ first, then repeat at $3/4$. Both weights were already used in the precursor. On this fixed spatial domain the square-root case is a valid measurement convention, even though the asymptotic contradiction in the source uses a test exponent strictly above the critical line.

Four nonoverlapping bands partition the parent interval up to endpoints. We also registered a fifth placement, $[344,688]$, whose log midpoint is the log of $344\sqrt 2\simeq486.4895$. It is near the known pointwise peak at $487$. The exact start that would center that peak is $487/\sqrt{2}\simeq344.3610$. Choosing $344$ fixes a simple pair of integer endpoints and makes the distinction between a targeted band and the optimizing band observable. The prior peak location determines the targeted choice, so its performance is descriptive. We additionally optimize over all real starts, including irrational ones. Reporting these as different comparisons avoids treating a deliberately targeted placement as a blind discovery.

\subsection{Certificate on each prime-power gap}

On an open interval containing no active prime-power event the sum has the form $G(y)=Ay-B$, where
\[
 A=c_4+\sum_{n\leq y}\chi_4(n)\Lambda(n)/n,
 \qquad B=\sum_{n\leq y}\chi_4(n)\Lambda(n).
\]
When $y$ reaches $n=p^k$, the two coefficients change by $\chi_4(n)\log(p)/n$ and $\chi_4(n)\log(p)$, respectively. The new term vanishes at $y=n$, giving continuity even though the slope can jump. Powers of two make zero contribution for this character. The implementation independently enumerates odd prime powers by trial division and by a sieve and checks equality of the resulting event dictionaries. There are 531 open affine cells in the parent interval. Within a cell the positive part of an affine function is supported on the entire interval, the empty set, or one side of its single possible zero. This reduction accounts for the full continuum between prime events.

For $0<\theta<1$ an antiderivative on a positive portion is
\[
 P_{A,B,\theta}(y)
   =y^{-\theta}\left(\frac{Ay}{1-\theta}+\frac{B}{\theta}\right).
\]
Differentiating yields $(Ay-B)y^{-1-\theta}$, with the minus sign on $B$ as required. For certified coefficient intervals $A^-,A^+$ and $B^-,B^+$ form the two affine functions $A^- y-B^+$ and $A^+y-B^-$. They bound $G$ for every positive $y$ in the cell. Clip each separately at zero, finding its root as an exact rational whenever it changes sign. Monotonicity of positive part then gives the mass enclosure on the cell. The same roots give the lengths of the positive portions in logarithmic coordinates. Such lengths are bounded by integrating $dy/y$ on the lower and upper positive portions, respectively. This evaluation never has to decide the exact sign of $G$ at an irrational root.

All logarithms and rational powers used in a decision are enclosed with outward integer arithmetic on a grid of spacing $2^{-160}$. Square and fourth roots use integer square roots with endpoint checks. A stored antiderivative difference can subtract two much larger close terms. The enclosing intervals propagate that loss of precision through both boundary evaluations. Summing cell contributions forward gives certified prefix integrals. Subtracting prefixes yields independent outward errors at the two ends and therefore a slightly wider interval than a direct sum over that band. The agreement with independent integral controls below checks that the resulting precision suffices. Enclosures are available in the archive to twelve printed decimal places. Digits in the printed tables are rounded for reading.

The normalized positive duration of a band is its positive log length divided by $\log2$. Its average height when positive is its mass divided by that positive log length. Multiplying these quantities by the window width recovers its mass. This identity lets us distinguish an interval which is frequently positive from an interval where its positive excursions reach high values. Since multiplying by $y^{-\theta}$ preserves sign, the positive duration must be identical at both exponents. The data satisfy this constraint even though the ordering of some band masses changes.

The third band carries 36.73 percent although it cannot contain the peak. Its positive average height is about $0.05579$, versus $0.05148$ in the first band, and it spends about twelve additional percentage points of logarithmic time above zero. Its large square-root-scale mass comes in part from having positive values during more than 95 percent of the log interval. The largest pointwise peak in the parent interval is approximately $0.12991743$. Assigning this height to an entire band would give an upper allowance of $0.12991743\log2$, about 113.33 percent of the \emph{whole parent's} observed mass. This bound is mathematically sound and loses nearly all resolving power here. The measurements exhibit separately how a global ceiling and the integral under it can differ.

\section{The best and worst placements over all real starts}
\label{sec:band-prime-scan}

The placement problem has a low-dimensional geometric structure. On the log axis write $H_\theta(y)=[G(y)]_+/y^\theta$. Translating a window a small distance removes a strip at its left end and adds one at its right. More precisely, continuity of $G$ and the fundamental theorem of calculus give
\[
 sM'_\theta(s)=H_\theta(2s)-H_\theta(s).
\]
The moving-band mass is continuously differentiable even at a kink of an endpoint prime sum. At a positive-edge interior extremum the two endpoint heights balance. Nothing in this equation requires the largest interior height to sit at the geometric midpoint. The relevant marginal comparison is what the window is about to gain or lose.

To cover the continuum of starts, split $[256,2048]$ at every prime-power knot for $G(s)$ and every half-knot for $G(2s)$. On each resulting gap let $G(s)=A_1s-B_1$ and $G(2s)=2A_2s-B_2$, and put $c=2^\theta$. The untruncated comparison is
\[
 D(s)=G(2s)-cG(s)=(2A_2-cA_1)s-(B_2-cB_1).
\]
If $D(s)\geq0$, monotonicity and homogeneity of positive part give $[G(2s)]_+\geq c[G(s)]_+$. The reverse weak inequality follows when $D(s)\leq0$. On any interval of constant weak sign, $M_\theta$ is monotone, with equality allowed where both endpoints are nonpositive. Consequently the global extrema are achieved within a finite list consisting of domain endpoints, breakpoints and interior roots of $D$. Adding breakpoints to this list does no harm even when the comparison preserves its sign across one. There is also no need to insert all zeros of $G$ into the placement search. Their clipping is already accounted for exactly in the integral evaluator.

This statement concerns attainment of extreme \emph{values}. A plateau could contain infinitely many optimal starts even if the candidate list is finite. The code therefore retains every candidate whose enclosure meets the extremal enclosure. A claim of a unique interior optimizer additionally needs a genuine slope crossing and strictly positive endpoint heights near that root. The program tests the signs of the affine comparisons on both sides. It encloses both untruncated endpoint heights on the entire root box and checks that their lower bounds exceed zero. This last test makes the derivative strictly signed immediately to either side of a crossing, except at the root itself. With all other candidates strictly excluded, monotonicity between candidates rules out an unseen plateau attaining the same value.

All 752 merged gaps had resolved endpoint comparisons for both exponents. There were 753 distinct breakpoints including the domain ends, and four interior roots of the untruncated comparison for each exponent. Where the signs change, the slope excludes zero. Dividing the outward offset enclosure by the outward slope enclosure encloses the true root in $[l,r]$ strictly inside its original gap. For any start in that tiny box the corresponding band contains $[r,2l]$ and is contained in $[l,2r]$. Integrating the positive measure on the inner and outer intervals then bounds its mass without assuming that an approximate root is the exact location. Each distinct extremum below retained one strict candidate among 757, with a certified positive normalized height at both edges.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}clrrr@{}}
\toprule
$\theta$ & Placement & Start (rounded) & Positive mass & Share of parent\\
\midrule
$1/2$ & maximum & 364.81964838 & 0.03773079957 & $47.48374447\%$\\
      & minimum & 634.70579760 & 0.00379230156 & $4.77256460\%$\\
$3/4$ & maximum & 363.99566147 & 0.00794381756 & $55.00306159\%$\\
      & minimum & 638.55063816 & 0.00071130409 & $4.92507565\%$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

At exponent $1/2$, starts in $[364.819648384,364.819648385]$ and $[634.705797598,634.705797599]$ enclose the maximum and minimum locations. Their mass ratio is about $9.9493$ for the best to worst placement. Both windows have precisely the same length in the measure used by the analytic criterion. The maximizing band contains the peak at $487$, and its left start is about $20.46$ units above the start that would center that peak. The targeted band captures approximately $98.76\%$ of the maximum possible mass. Its success is real but local: shifts of the same band length can carry less than a tenth as much. The endpoint height at the maximum is about $0.00750848$, far below the global pointwise peak. The endpoint height at the minimum is about $0.03787976$, considerably higher. Even the common height at both edges of a stationary band does not rank its interior integral.

One way to read the table is as an exact worst-case loss for a finite reporting problem. An observer who can inspect the parent interval everywhere except one unknown contiguous factor-two band could miss at most $47.48375\%$ of the positive square-root-weighted area on this specific curve. The worst band realizes that allowance, while the best possible placement of the omission would lose only about $4.77256\%$. The full original signal is known here, which is precisely what permits the sharp calculation. An arithmetic construction with unknown ordinates would need an independently proved upper bound on that omission cost. The observed positive mass outside a chosen band neither controls its unknown interior nor certifies the asymptotic behavior of new parent windows.
