Each curve joins sampled shares at starts spaced by 16 in the original scale; the filled extrema are determined separately over every real start. A horizontal reference marks the common fraction of logarithmic width.}\end{figure}

\subsection{Cross-checks with different failure modes}

The fixed-band checks use a second quadrature independent of the antiderivative formula and its sign-root calculation. This method builds its own gap list from sieved prime powers, including zero-weight events. Within each gap it partitions into short intervals with increment at most approximately $y/4096$. Since $G$ is affine there, its positive area with respect to $dy$ is the sum of exact triangles or trapezoids for the endpoint bracketing functions. Multiply the lower and upper positive areas by the minimum and maximum of the decreasing weight $y^{-1-\theta}$ on the subinterval. Addition gives an outer enclosure of the weighted integral. In $[344,688]$ at the square-root scale the mesh method gives an interval from $0.037255275$ to $0.037268712$, containing the much narrower analytic primitive bracket. All ten comparisons contain their corresponding primary enclosures. Both methods share directed elementary logarithms and a mathematical normalization, so this is independence of quadrature and event representation, not a second independent proof of the formula for $c_4$.

After the primary extrema were obtained, we also checked them with a second search that uses no derivative comparison, critical locations or optimizer candidates. For starts in any interval $[l,r]$ with $r<2l$, inner and outer bands bracket all the possible positive masses by inclusion. Starting with a coarse partition of the whole start axis, keep best and worst sampled integrals as one-sided witnesses. Split a box if its outer integral still exceeds the witnessed maximum by more than $10^{-6}$, or if its inner integral still falls short of the witnessed minimum by more than the same amount. Otherwise certify that box and move to another. Such discarded boxes remain certified when the witnessed maximum later increases or the witnessed minimum decreases. This construction produces a covering by tested boxes and a uniform enclosure on the global extrema.

At exponent $1/2$ the procedure visited 692 boxes and terminated with 360 in its covering; the deepest subdivision used twelve bisections. It placed the maximum in $[0.037730799468,0.037731732888]$ and the minimum in $[0.003791346359,0.003792301575]$. At exponent $3/4$ it visited 328 boxes, ending with 178. The value enclosures there were $[0.007943817552,0.007944763998]$ and $[0.000710316080,0.000711304127]$. All four contain the corresponding primary result. The second search shares an integrator with the main search. Its coverage argument is different: positivity and interval inclusion replace all assumptions about the signs of endpoint slopes. Its precision is deliberately coarser; it is an independent challenge to missing a large excursion between candidates.

\subsection{The ranking transition between the first and third bands}

The two exponents in the protocol produced an observable change in the ordering of the early and late bands. Increasing $\theta$ tilts the weighting toward small $y$. At $\theta=1/2$ the third aligned band beats the first; at $\theta=3/4$ the first beats the third. After recording this comparison we asked where the two cross. Let $E=[256,512]$ and $L=[1024,2048]$ and denote their positive masses by $m_E(\theta)$ and $m_L(\theta)$. Both are strictly positive. On each finite interval form the probability distribution whose density is its positive mass element divided by that interval's positive mass. Differentiation under the finite integral is justified by a uniform integrable bound on each compact exponent range. It gives
\[
 \frac{d}{d\theta}\log\!\left(\frac{m_E(\theta)}{m_L(\theta)}\right)
 = \mathbb{E}_{L,\theta}[\log y]-\mathbb{E}_{E,\theta}[\log y].
\]
Every point of $L$ is at least twice every point of $E$, and their farthest endpoint ratio is eight. The derivative is consequently between $\log2$ and $\log8$. This proves strict increase without assumptions about the shape of the oscillations. Since the endpoint comparisons have opposite signs, there is exactly one crossing. The underlying measured function remains identical throughout. The change in rank is generated by the analytic weighting alone.

To locate the crossing, the supplementary control evaluated the closed integral again at dyadic exponents between $1/2$ and $3/4$. If $\theta=m/2^k$, $y^\theta$ can be computed as a product of the corresponding repeated square roots of $y$. Bounding each square root outwards gives a directed enclosure for the power using the same integer grid. Twenty-four bisections with resolved mass differences give
\[
 \frac{23991697}{33554432}
 < \theta_{E=L} <
 \frac{47983395}{67108864},
 \qquad 0.715008288 < \theta_{E=L} < 0.715008304 .
\]
At the lower rational endpoint the early-minus-late difference is below $-8.3620\times10^{-12}$; at the upper endpoint it exceeds $1.010265\times10^{-10}$, with nonoverlapping signed enclosures in the receipt. This result is scoped to the two specified aligned bands. It does not assert that their crossing governs the optimum among all continuously placed bands. In fact the continuous maximum on either endpoint exponent remains near a start of $364$. Distinguishing those two optimization problems prevents a legitimate ranking transition from being misread as a discontinuous change in the underlying arithmetic.

\subsection{What this finite result contributes upstream}

The rate employed in the source criterion balances accumulated positive area against a power of the window start. The measured value of $T_{1/2}$ here would supply only one finite data point for such an asymptotic criterion. There is, however, a precise obstruction to a tempting shortcut: a proposed formula assigning area in proportion to logarithmic length would give exactly $25\%$ in every one of these bands. Two bands with that length attain about $47.48\%$ and $4.77\%$. Replacing a continuous-integral question by the maximum at one ordinate fares differently but also loses essential information. Among the four aligned placements the largest pointwise peak resides in the first interval, while the largest square-root-weighted area resides in the third. These two failures are direct finite measurements of the very statistic occurring in the analytic criterion.

An estimate that uses both length and a suitable upper envelope still survives every one of these tests. Its usefulness depends on whether the arithmetic furnishes that envelope specifically on the exceptional part, and whether it does so along arbitrarily late windows for one fixed family. Our sharp finite maximum is the ceiling in a problem where the signal has already been specified everywhere and the exceptional band has a prescribed shape. It does not constrain exceptions arising from a missing upstream premise. It instead identifies an explicit benchmark that a proposed estimate should handle. A claim based solely on the number or placement of exceptional integers has additional work before it can be read as a statement about the positive prime-sum measure.

Finally the positive measure used here has a useful additivity that a count of failures lacks. For overlapping candidate bands one must integrate the union or subtract its intersection; summing their separate allowances can count the same prime-sum area twice. The first four registered bands provide a direct conservation check through their disjoint interiors. The near-centered band illustrates the other case, covering the high part of two adjacent aligned bands. This distinction matters if a source trace later produces several overlapping exceptional regions on the same arithmetic window.


\section{What the prime sum itself can pay across a gap}
\label{sec:curvature}
The preceding alternatives act on arithmetic information: one tries to keep a certificate at the original ordinate or to move the comparison interval. Here we use a feature specific to the observable. It is a sum of ramps with known kinks. Its values at two points on opposite sides of a hole constrain how far it can rise between them. The result gives an explicit sufficient condition with measurable inputs. It requires the actual scalar comparison at both ends; knowing that an exceptional integer misses those endpoints is a different premise.

\subsection{Remove the affine ambiguity first}
Fix a finite, positively weighted family \(F\); write \(A=\sum_i a_i\), and \(P\) for the sum of the weights of principal members. For \(y>1\) the observable has the real representation
\begin{equation}
 G_F(y)=\alpha_F y-Py\log y+\sum_{2\le n\le y} j_n(y-n),
 \qquad j_n={\Lambda(n)\over n}\sum_i a_i\,\Re\chi_i(n).
 \label{eq:ramp}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Lambda(p^k)=\log p\) and it vanishes on other integers. The constant \(\alpha_F\) incorporates the principal normalization and the logarithmic derivatives at one of the nonprincipal members. In particular \(|j_n|\le A\log n/n\). These facts come directly from the pinned ramp and family definitions. The chord argument below remains valid with any additional affine function, so its conclusions do not depend on the numerical approximation to \(\alpha_F\). Measuring the actual positive mass later in this paper \emph{does} depend on that constant. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean#L10-L36}{[Original ramp and normalization]}

On an interval \(1<a<b\), let \(T G\) be the straight line through \((a,G(a))\) and \((b,G(b))\), and put \(L=b-a\). For \(z,y\in[a,b]\) define
\[
 K(y,z)=\frac{(\min(y,z)-a)(b-\max(y,z))}{b-a}.
\]
It vanishes if either argument is an endpoint. As a function of \(y\) it is a tent with its peak at \(z\). A ramp \((y-z)_+\) lies below its straight chord by exactly \(K(y,z)\); this follows by evaluating the two affine pieces on either side of \(z\). A ramp with \(z\le a\) is already affine on the interval, and a ramp starting at or beyond \(b\) contributes zero to the difference. This explains why only the \emph{interior} prime powers enter the bill for losing an interval. Kinks at the endpoints change an adjacent slope but leave this particular chord intact.

Since the second derivative of \(y\log y\) is \(1/y\), integration of the same tent identity gives
\begin{equation}
 G_F(y)-T G_F(y)=-\sum_{a<n<b}j_n K(y,n)
                       +P\int_a^b K(y,z)\frac{dz}{z}.
 \label{eq:green}
\end{equation}
For example, an elementary verification of the integral term splits the integral at \(y\), differentiates twice, and checks that the result has second derivative \(-1/y\) and vanishes at \(a,b\). Those conditions uniquely determine the departure of \(-y\log y\) from its chord. The downward jumps of slope (\(j_n<0\)) can raise the sum above the chord. Upward jumps can only lower this difference, although they can of course raise \(G_F\) itself. The distinction concerns comparison with the two fixed endpoints.

\subsection{An upper bound with quantities one can compute}
Suppose \(0<\theta<1\), \(C\ge0\), and the true sum satisfies \(G_F(a)\le Ca^\theta\), \(G_F(b)\le Cb^\theta\). The concavity of \(y^\theta\) means \(T G_F(y)\le C y^\theta\) throughout. Taking the positive part, dropping the favorable jumps, and integrating in the original measure gives
\begin{align}
 \int_a^b [G_F(y)]_+\,\frac{dy}{y^{1+\theta}}
 &\le C\log(b/a)+{\cal D}_\theta(a,b),\label{eq:chordcap}\\
 {\cal D}_\theta(a,b)&=
 a^{-1-\theta}\left\{
 \frac12\sum_{a<n<b}[-j_n]_+(n-a)(b-n)
 +\frac{P L^3}{12a}\right\}. \label{eq:curvaturecap}
\end{align}
To obtain the discrete term use \(\int_a^b K(y,n)dy=(n-a)(b-n)/2\). For the continuous term use \(1/z\le1/a\) and integrate this area in \(z\) a second time. The triangular area is largest for a kink at the midpoint and is at most \(L^2/8\). We have deliberately bounded \(y^{-1-\theta}\) by its value at the lower endpoint. On a wide interval retaining the weight in the integral of \(K\) gives a stronger computable bound. The cap shown has the advantage that only a weighted list of downward slope jumps is needed. No values of \(G_F\) inside the gap enter its premises.

The weights \((n-a)(b-n)\) contain information lost by counting prime powers. A jump very near an endpoint has little leverage after both endpoints have been fixed. The maximum factor \(L^2/8\) is attained for a single jump at the midpoint in the unweighted area problem. Cancellation between upward and downward jumps can make the cap substantially larger than the actual positive departure. It is legitimate to discard that cancellation for an upper bound; a sharp mass prediction generally requires keeping it. The code tests the sign by comparing a direct prime-power ramp to a separate Green-tent sum at every event and intervening midpoint. Equality on the one-jump control and the vanishing upper error for the positive jump give tests that would reverse under a sign mistake.

This is a conditional statement for the real arithmetic observable, including a principal term when present. It also identifies a regime where no endpoint error is needed: if \(P=0\) and every effective jump in a gap is nonnegative, the unnormalized sum is convex there. Its chord lies above it and the two endpoint comparisons extend throughout. This special case need not occur in a large family window. It demonstrates that ignorance of a cohomological certificate at a real ordinate is logically distinct from ignorance of all analytic structure of the sum.

\subsection{An exponent test for several holes}
Let the outer window be \([Y,Y^\lambda]\) with fixed \(\lambda>1\). Assume the same endpoint ceiling \(C y^\theta\) holds on its certified portion, including both ends of each omitted component \((a_i,b_i)\). For this calculation take finitely many disjoint components. Set \(u_i=\log(b_i/a_i)\) and assume \(u_i\le1\). The number of interior integers is at most \(L_i+1\), where \(L_i=b_i-a_i\), and \([-j_n]_+\le A(\log b_i)/a_i\). Thus a convenient uniform bound is
\begin{align}
 {\cal D}_\theta(a_i,b_i)\le {}&
 {A\log b_i\over8}a_i^{-2-\theta}(L_i^3+L_i^2)
 +{P\over12}a_i^{-2-\theta}L_i^3 .\label{eq:trivialjumpbound}
\end{align}
The extra \(L_i^2\) matters. An interval of length less than one can still contain an integer, and if it contains one downward kink its departure above the chord is quadratic in the ordinary width. For \(u_i\le1\), \(L_i=a_i(e^{u_i}-1)\le (e-1)a_i u_i\). Summing \eqref{eq:trivialjumpbound} and adding the good part gives
\begin{align}
 W_\theta(Y) \le {}& C(\lambda-1)\log Y \nonumber\\
 &+O_{F,\lambda}\!\left((1+\log Y)
   \left[Y^{\lambda(1-\theta)}\sum_i u_i^3
                       +Y^{-\theta}\sum_i u_i^2\right]\right).
 \label{eq:cubic}
\end{align}
The constant is independent of the positions and the number of intervals. The first term on the right uses the baseline \(C\) over the entire window, including the holes. The other terms cover departures above that baseline. Endpoint values can be negative, and the deduction still applies. A hole touching an uncertified outer endpoint cannot be charged to this formula until a second endpoint has been justified or the window shortened.

For instance, \(\sum_i u_i\le Y^{-d}\), \(d>0\), implies \(\sum_i u_i^3\le Y^{-3d}\) and \(\sum_i u_i^2\le Y^{-2d}\). On arbitrarily late windows with the stated endpoint hypotheses, it suffices that
\[
                    3d\ \ge\ \lambda(1-\theta).
\]
At equality the bound grows at most logarithmically. For the illustrative \(\lambda=3/2,\theta=5/8\) this asks for \(d\ge3/16\). The elementary bound in equation~\eqref{eq:band-elem}, charging \( |G_F(y)|/y^\theta\ll_F y^{1-\theta}\log(2y)\) uniformly to the missing length would require \(d\ge9/16\) in the same calculation. The improved sufficient condition uses the locations of the two known endpoint values. Neither threshold claims optimality for actual character sums.

A bound on total length is just one usable corollary. For a specified collection the sum of cubes is a better statistic. Splitting a single gap into \(k\) equal ones with newly established endpoints divides the cubic cost by \(k^2\) and its quadratic correction by \(k\). This operation adds mathematical information: subdividing the interval on paper without proving new endpoint estimates buys nothing. In particular an arbitrary arithmetic exceptional band can have macroscopic logarithmic width. The source gives no \(Y^{-d}\) bound for the whole of such a band on a prescribed window. The calculation should guide the search for additional endpoint information, and should not be substituted for it.

\subsection{The sign test at 487}
On \([486,488]\) there is one interior prime power, the prime \(487\equiv3\bmod4\). For the actual \(\chi_4\), \(j_{487}=-(\log487)/487\). The difference from the chord is exactly a positive tent. Its unweighted area is \(\log487/(2\cdot487)\); its weighted positive area at \(\theta=1/2\) is enclosed in
\[
        [0.00000059117630,\;0.00000059117631].
\]
On \([508,510]\) the sole jump comes from \(509\equiv1\bmod4\). The tent is below its chord throughout and its positive departure is identically zero. On \([488,490]\) the only interior integer is \(489=3\cdot163\), which is no prime power, and the sum is its chord exactly. All three intervals have ordinary width two. Their different behavior is completely predicted by the sign and presence of a jump. The earlier measurement of the peak at 487 informed the choice of this first control.

\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
Interval & Weighted area above chord & Fraction of unweighted cap used\\ \midrule
\([256,512]\)    & 0.00197440 & 1.18071\%\\
\([512,1024]\)   & 0.00005841 & 0.02210\%\\
\([1024,2048]\)  & 0.03631055 & 6.92198\%\\
\([2048,4096]\)  & 0.00087658 & 0.17346\%\\
\([471,503]\)    & 0.00047197 & 100.00000\%\\
\([455,519]\)    & 0.00278854 & 82.25239\%\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Directions of curvature for the actual modulo-four ramp. The middle column integrates the positive departure from the straight chord through the actual endpoint values. The last column compares the unweighted positive departure to the unweighted downward-jump budget. They are different statistics. The archive contains outward enclosures for every row.}
\label{tab:chord}
\end{table}

The third dyadic interval has a substantially larger weighted departure from its chord than the other aligned intervals. Its chord can lie below zero, so an area measured above that chord can exceed the area above zero. This resolves what might otherwise look like an inconsistency when comparing this table to the prime-mass results. The fraction of the curvature cap used ranges from about \(0.0221\%\) on the second aligned band to equality on the single-sign controls. A relation that is locally an equality can be a poor numerical estimate on a broad interval after signs have been discarded. The 4,950 rational single-kink cases check both the integrated tent factor and its midpoint maximum.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\bandfigure{.96\linewidth}{tents}\caption{Exact shapes on the two single-jump controls. Each panel scales the vertical axis by the magnitude of its own central deviation; the chord residual has a different sign although the two measurement intervals have the same width.}\end{figure}

The principal smooth term was tested separately after the prime controls. Its unweighted departure has the exact integral \((b^2-a^2)/4-(ab/2)\log(b/a)\). Integrating the positive kernel between the constant density bounds \(1/b\) and \(1/a\) sandwiches this number between \(L^3/(12b)\) and \(L^3/(12a)\). Outward arithmetic strictly certified both comparisons at four scales. For \([486,488]\), the smooth area is approximately \(0.00136892655\), below the allowance \(0.00137174212\). The smooth test isolates the continuous curvature. All prime-power rows in Table~\ref{tab:chord} use the nonprincipal character and have \(P=0\).

\subsection{Where the proposed replacement stops}
Equations \eqref{eq:chordcap}--\eqref{eq:cubic} answer a deliberately intermediate question. They would let an arithmetic proof leave small holes if it could certify sufficient endpoints for a fixed \(F\). There are two ways an attempted application can fail. The local cohomological and explicit-formula data may fail to assemble a scalar estimate even at an apparently favorable endpoint. Alternatively, the resulting certified endpoints may straddle a band so wide that even the curvature budget is too large. A source trace establishing pointwise compatibility at some intermediate stage does not by itself settle either issue. Conversely, a synthetic unbounded pulse satisfying a weakened scalar implication is usually not a sum of prime-power ramps. Such a counterexample diagnoses loss of information at an interface, while \eqref{eq:green} states one piece that the genuine arithmetic family always retains.

The parallel conclusions answer different questions about the same missing interval. The arithmetic calculation can sometimes certify points previously discarded. The chord calculation prices the remaining interval when its endpoints are known. Their combination would require bookkeeping of which original family, character, prime pair and scale occur at each handoff. In this paper an inequality valid after such bookkeeping is reported as conditional until that handoff has been supplied. This boundary is essential to interpreting any improvement in a sufficient exponent.


\section{Which information would actually replace the clean-window argument?}
The calculations give a decision procedure for assessing a proposed arithmetic shortcut. It starts by fixing the family and exponent. Is there an arbitrarily late supply of windows on which the scalar comparison is proved at every ordinate? If so the previous positive-mass argument applies immediately. A proof using an improved local weight allowance can reach this same interface provided all the compatibility and range obligations are transported. If only part of the window is certified, the next step depends on what is known about the remainder.

\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}p{.22\linewidth}YY@{}}
\toprule
Information available & Consequence & Remaining arithmetic obligation\\ \midrule
Cardinality of exceptional integers and control of window location & A clean power window exists above the proved geometric budget. & Show the original hypotheses on the same window and preserve the character family.\\
Good complement and two height bounds & Integrating the two ceilings gives the sharp scalar-envelope allowance. & Control the height on the exceptional ordinates and the logarithmic length they occupy.\\
A bounded number of bad bands and a fixed good fraction & Some clean subinterval has fixed positive power width. & Establish a scalar comparison on that subinterval at arbitrarily large starts.\\
Known scalar values at both ends of each hole & The weighted downward-jump budget in \eqref{eq:curvaturecap} controls area above the endpoint ceiling. & Produce eligible endpoints with small aggregate curvature cost on cofinal windows.\\
A finite prime certificate on a known interval & Its mass and extremizing band can be computed with outward error bounds. & To exclude a zero one still needs a statement about unboundedly many scales.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Different sufficient routes carry different information. The proposed replacements for the original selection must supply the obligations in the last column.}
\end{table}

\subsection{How much area could one afford to lose?}
For one concrete scale comparison imagine a zero with real part \(3/4\), take \(\theta=5/8\), and use the original final width \(\lambda=3/2\). The forced-mass threshold in exponent is \(3/16\): for every fixed \(0<b<3/16\) the mass eventually exceeds \(Y^b\). Thus even some polynomial error can be tolerated when excluding this particular zero. A proved cofinal upper bound of order \(Y^{1/8}\log^kY\), for fixed \(k\), would suffice by choosing \(b\) strictly between \(1/8\) and \(3/16\). To exclude every zero immediately to the right of a fixed test line \(\theta\), no fixed positive exponent loss works in that comparison. This dependence on the target explains why the original proof freely absorbs logarithms while carefully tracking every positive power.

Consider an attempted comparison that supplies a common endpoint ceiling and a total missing logarithmic length at most \(Y^{-d}\). Applying \eqref{eq:cubic} to this example gives a possible mass exponent of \(\max(0,9/16-3d)\), up to logarithms. For the specific hypothetical zero at \(3/4\), already \(d>1/8\) would put this upper exponent strictly below \(3/16\). For a subpower upper bound, \(d\ge3/16\) suffices. A third situation would be to use the geometric result to move off the bad bands, spending no mass on them. Which route is least demanding depends on whether the source provides upper scalar values, the number and placement of components, or the ability to choose a sufficiently late new interval. This is why simply reporting the proportion of points that pass a test is inadequate.

\subsection{What the data can reject}
A uniform allocation rule charging a quarter of the full prime mass to every quarter of the log axis fails in an exactly specified example. The best and worst factor-two bands give almost a tenfold ratio at the square-root scale. The aligned band that contains the largest pointwise value loses the integrated comparison to one that does not contain it. These results rule out two specific inferences about the measured signal. They do not show that a source-generated band will hit the maximizer, and they do not show that the cohomological defect caused the positive mass in any of the tested bands. It is even possible for weight-allowance failure and zero area to coexist in an abstract comparison. The improved arithmetic thresholds likewise do not say where the true positive mass is concentrated.

The two controlled computations tell us what to record when an actual arithmetic instance becomes accessible. The real interval endpoints and the character-family identity should be recorded before the band is inspected. The source of each arithmetic endpoint certificate, including the local host if used, must accompany a claim based on interpolation. An area on a synthetic band occupying the same amount of log time is a control for the numerical procedure. Matching its width is insufficient for matching its mass. A direct comparison could use the extremum enclosures in this study as a finite outer reference in the special case of the same character, base window, normalization and factor-two shape.

\subsection{The open mathematical handoff}
The most informative open problem is to assemble the improved active-weight estimates into the original family construction and identify the set of ordinates on which all the remaining host comparisons hold. An accessible first case would transport the moving-field bound with \(a<11/20\) through the pointwise even-layer and field adapters at one common ordinate; the sharper \(a<3/5\) calculation would then require retaining the earlier prime-pair estimate as well. To turn such a local result into a cofinal mass bound, its constants and the choice of fixed family must survive repeated selection of larger scales. The finite character computations do not provide those witnesses.

This proposal is testable in two directions. A successful transport would exhibit the specific local host inputs for each surviving ordinate and hence show which region is genuinely newly certified. If a later construction requires the stronger premise, the first such use would locate where the present recovery stops. Either outcome would improve our understanding of why the existing proof builds a whole clean window. This study leaves both the realized exceptional integers and the cofinal incidence of missing scalar endpoints open.

\appendix
\section{Verification, reproduction and evidence}
The fork's comparison base is \texttt{08188ff}; the mathematical source for the trace is commit \texttt{40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005}. The preceding experiment at \texttt{b7b5bc6} supplies the previously replayed positive-mass consequence, the finite baseline, and the original interval arithmetic used here. Two Python modules for the prime computation are vendored byte-for-byte to keep the package locally runnable. Every new floating display in the paper rounds a more precise certificate or labels an illustrative plot. The finite prime decisions use integer and rational interval arithmetic. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/tree/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005}{[Original source pin]} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/tree/b7b5bc6e5e6814f654753fcbd5f45fecf370344c/research/uriel/experiments/zero-and-arithmetic-windows}{[Previous research package]}

Seven standard-library programs reproduce the finite claims: the original-source ablation controls, two geometry programs, three prime-mass programs, and the chord program. Their JSON outputs are compared to the archived receipts. A separate optional pass authenticates 25 original source files and 45 pinned ranges for the arithmetic trace, and four files and four ranges for the interval geometry. These lists overlap and account for 26 distinct original source files in total. A successful hash check says that a cited range has the expected bytes; it does not by itself check any Lean proof.

Two supplemental modules compile with Lean 4.33.1 and Mathlib revision \href{https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/tree/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474}{\texttt{0df444a360ea}}. The replay records the declared axioms and calls the independent \texttt{leanchecker} executable on each compiled module. \texttt{BandBudget} checks the real-power envelope, absorption, the obstruction at an equality of exponents with adverse constants, active finite summation and the inequality giving the coarse moving-field threshold. \texttt{WindowGeometry} checks the hit criterion, the clean-window construction on real centers, overlap inequalities, a geometric obstruction, and feasibility at a given one-band overlap level. These ten reported declarations delimit the new formal work. The integer approximation for worst placements, the full arithmetic reassembly with altered weight bounds, the derivative-based prime integral computation, and the Green-kernel interpolation theorem are assessed by written arguments or by finite numerical certificates as indicated in the text. A full rebuild of the original RH development was not performed.

\subsection{Reproduction commands}
After extracting the full ZIP, change into the directory containing the paper. The core reproduction command is
\begin{quote}\ttfamily python3 -B evidence/reproduce.py\end{quote}
Use Python 3.11 or later. The default path needs no third-party packages. The command compares the stored file checksums first. To verify the pinned source ranges as well, pass \texttt{--source} followed by the path to a checkout of the original source commit. For both kernel replays add \texttt{--toolchain} and \texttt{--mathlib}, each followed by the location of the corresponding Lean SDK and built Mathlib checkout. The two mathematical supplements import Mathlib. The full original project's object cache is unnecessary for these two replays. Machine-readable provenance and a claim ledger appear alongside the manuscript.

Compile the manuscript using \texttt{latexmk -lualatex} with the TeX filename as argument. Only the one TeX file is needed for the paper. It contains the vector figures and a compressed copy of the evidence directory, including its runner. The full ZIP exposes the same evidence as ordinary editable files. The optional figure renderer, with ReportLab 4.4.9, reads the archived data and writes the vector illustrations. The PDF is provided for reading independently of either software installation.

\subsection{Primary protocols and later checks}
The four primary protocols distinguish earlier knowledge from new predictions. They are reproduced in the package and archived in the first experiment-branch commit. The protocols were written locally before each component's new runs; their publication commit followed laboratory work already underway. The arithmetic prime-pair refinement, the bounded-component gap inequality, the derivative-free extrema search, the crossover calculation and the smooth chord control are separately identified in the evidence history. In particular the secondary search was designed after the primary extrema were seen. It has a different mathematical failure mode and shares the positive-integral implementation. This status matters more for evaluating independence than the number of agreement digits.

The continuous extrema are deterministic statements over \emph{all} permitted real starts, while the finite mesh serves only as an illustration and a check. The optimizer enclosures use the shared normalization, so independent quadrature does not vouch independently for that normalization. The formal source is authoritative for the named original declaration; a written derivation using several such declarations is an additional mathematical argument. The actual finite intervals, synthetic forbidden bands, and hypothetical zeros play different roles throughout this work.
\end{document}
